It wasn’t a big dramatic hobby, but it helped me feel connected to my body.\n\nAnd my garden—usually I can spend ages out there fussing over little things, pulling weeds, checking what’s blooming, moving pots around. I used to take bird photos too. I’d keep my camera ready because if I saw something in the yard or on a walk, I’d get excited about trying to capture it.\n\nBut lately I just… don’t. I keep canceling plans or telling myself I’ll go later, and then later never happens. My camera has basically been sitting in the closet, and I’ll look at it and think, “I should take it out,” but it feels like this enormous task for no real payoff. Even things I know I used to enjoy feel like they’re asking too much of me now, or like they’re not worth the effort.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or worried a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I do worry about things sometimes—work stuff, money, whether I’m keeping up with everything—but I don’t know if I’d say it’s anxiety exactly, or if it’s just that everything feels harder so normal worries feel louder.\n\nIt’s not like I’m walking around panicking all day, if that’s what you mean. I don’t think I’ve noticed that as the main thing. It’s more like… I get stuck thinking about how behind I am or how much I’m not doing, and then I feel overwhelmed. But I’m not sure whether that counts as being anxious or if it’s just part of feeling run down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. I keep trying to think of something that I genuinely look forward to, and it’s hard to come up with anything that feels… real.\n\nLike, hiking used to be something I would actually plan around. I’d check the weather, think about which trail I wanted to do, maybe get my bag ready the night before. Yoga was similar—not always exciting exactly, but I knew I’d feel better after, so I’d make time for it. And my garden used to pull me outside without me even thinking about it. I’d notice one little thing that needed doing and then suddenly I’d been out there for an hour.\n\nNow it’s like I can technically remember enjoying those things, but I don’t feel the pull toward them. I’ll tell myself, “Maybe I’ll go for a hike this weekend,” and then when the time comes I cancel or just don’t go. My camera has been sitting in the closet, too. I used to love taking bird photos, and now I’ll see it there and think I should take it out, but it feels like too much effort for something that probably won’t feel worth it anyway.\n\nSo… I guess there are things I used to enjoy, but lately I’m not really finding pleasure in them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that clearly. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it very well. Some nights I know I’m tired, and I’ll tell myself I should go to bed, but I don’t always have a good sense of whether I’m actually sleeping badly or just feeling worn out in general.\n\nI haven’t noticed some big, obvious pattern that I could point to, like, “Oh, every night I’m awake until this exact time,” or anything like that. It’s more that my days feel kind of blurred lately, so when you ask about sleep I have to stop and think, “Wait, am I sleeping okay?” And I honestly don’t know. I might be sleeping less or not sleeping deeply, but I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed anything really clear with my appetite. It’s not something I’ve been paying close attention to, honestly.\n\nI mean, I eat, but some days it’s more just because it’s time to eat or because I realize I haven’t had anything in a while, not necessarily because I’m hungry in a normal way. But then other times I’ll snack on whatever’s easy, so it’s hard for me to say there’s a real pattern. I haven’t been tracking meals or weight or anything like that, so I’d be guessing a little.\n\nSo… maybe it’s a bit off, but I can’t point to a definite change like “I’m eating a lot more” or “I’m barely eating.” It’s more that I haven’t really been noticing food much either way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about not wanting to be alive or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve sat down and made some kind of plan or anything like that. I haven’t. But late at night, especially when everything is quiet and I’m just lying there with my thoughts, I’ll catch myself thinking that my husband might be better off without me. And then I sort of scare myself because it’s not just a random one-time thought. It comes back.\n\nSometimes it’s more like wishing I could just disappear or not have to be here dealing with myself, if that makes sense. And then I feel guilty for even thinking it, because I know how awful that would be for him, but in the moment my brain keeps going there anyway. So… yes, I’ve been having those thoughts, and the fact that they keep returning is what worries me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it really started around when we took on the mortgage… so, about two months ago now. That’s the point I keep coming back to when I try to make sense of it.\n\nBefore that, I was kind of in this very overcharged mode about work and business stuff, like pushing hard and feeling like I had to keep everything moving. And then after the mortgage happened, it’s like I swung the other way. I don’t know if it was the pressure of it or just the reality setting in, but since then my mood has been in this low stretch.\n\nSo… not forever, but not just a couple bad days either. About two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I didn’t really notice it at first, or maybe I didn’t want to notice it, but my husband has actually pointed it out a few times.\n\nHe’ll say I’m moving around the house like I’m underwater, which sounds dramatic, but I know what he means. Like I’ll get up to do something simple, even just walking from one room to another or putting something away, and I feel like everything takes this ridiculous amount of effort. I’m not rushing anywhere. I’m just sort of… slow. Heavy, almost.\n\nAnd with talking, that’s been happening too. He’ll ask me something very basic, like what I want for dinner or whether I saw some email, and I know I heard him, but it takes me a while to answer. It’s like the words are there somewhere, but they come out late and slow, and then I feel bad because he’s waiting and I can tell he’s trying not to get impatient.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have noticed it more because he’s noticed it. It’s not all the time every second, but lately it’s been enough that it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about not enjoying the things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel guilty, though I don’t know if it’s only about not enjoying things. It sort of turns into this bigger guilt spiral.\n\nLike, if I’m not enjoying something we used to do, or I’m just not able to be present for it, my mind goes straight back to the mortgage. I keep replaying that decision over and over, like I’m reviewing evidence against myself. I tell myself I was selfish, that I pushed us into something reckless, that I put our whole household at risk because I wanted things to move forward or because I thought I knew what I was doing.\n\nAnd my husband keeps reminding me, “We made that decision together,” which is objectively true. I know that. I can hear him say it, and part of me understands it. But emotionally it doesn’t land. I still feel like somehow it’s my fault, like I should have been the one to stop it or question it more or protect us from the pressure of it.\n\nSo when I’m not enjoying normal things, it just adds another layer. I think, great, not only did I put us in this position, now I can’t even be a decent partner and enjoy our life. It makes me feel ashamed, honestly. Like I’ve failed at something I’m supposed to be able to handle.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, so I could be off by a little. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, and nobody has said anything like, “Oh, you’ve lost weight,” so I don’t think there’s been some major drop.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure what counts as significant, but no, I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly lost a lot of weight recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… actually, yes. There was this burst where I really could not sit still. It wasn’t just normal “I should get things done” energy, it felt like I kept popping up out of my chair before I’d even finished whatever I was doing.\n\nI remember I kept jumping up to sketch out different office layouts, like I suddenly needed to figure out where everything should go, even though there was no real deadline or reason it had to be solved right then. Then I’d start reorganizing files, and before that was even finished I’d think of one more thing I should do, and then one more thing after that.\n\nIt was kind of scattered, but at the time it felt productive, like I was finally going to get everything in order. Looking back, it was a little much. I wasn’t calmly working through a list. I was just… up and down, up and down, starting tasks because I felt like I had to, not because they were actually urgent.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. My husband has actually pointed it out more than I have noticed it myself. He’ll say I’m moving around the house like I’m underwater, which is… embarrassing, but also kind of accurate.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing to move slowly. It feels like everything takes extra effort, even just walking from one room to another or getting something from the kitchen. I’ll realize I’ve been standing there for a second before actually doing the next thing, like my body is delayed.\n\nAnd when he asks me something simple, like what I want for dinner or whether I saw where something is, sometimes it takes me a while to answer. I know I should be able to just respond, but the words come out slowly, or I have to kind of gather them first. He’ll be waiting, and I can see him trying to be patient, but I know it’s noticeable.\n\nSo yes, most of the time lately I do seem slower — moving slower, talking slower — and it’s frustrating because inside I’m aware of it, but I can’t always make myself speed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly point to, no. Over the past two months I haven’t noticed my weight changing in any major way.\n\nI’m not someone who checks the scale consistently, so I can’t give you an exact number or say, “Oh, I lost five pounds,” or anything like that. I mostly notice through my clothes or if someone comments, and nothing has really stood out. My clothes fit about the same, as far as I can tell.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not completely sure, but I haven’t noticed any significant weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been fidgeting or having trouble staying still when sitting down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I know I kind of touched on this already, but it really was like I could not stay in the chair for more than a few minutes at a time.\n\nI’d sit down thinking I was going to do one thing, and then suddenly I’d be up again because I had this idea that I needed to sketch out a different office layout, like where desks or shelves should go, even though nobody had asked me to do that and there wasn’t any real reason it had to be handled right then. Then I’d sit down again, and almost immediately I’d think, no, I should reorganize these files first, or I should move this stack over there, or start some other little task.\n\nIt wasn’t just normal fidgeting, like tapping my foot. It was more like my whole body kept deciding I had to get up and do something. And at the time it felt like I was being productive, but looking back, I was just bouncing from one thing to another and couldn’t really stay still.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed myself sleeping more than usual, at least not in a way I could measure.

I mean, I’ve had times where I’m in bed longer or I’ll just sort of stay there because getting up feels like a lot, but I don’t know if that counts as actually sleeping more. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, that’s been happening. Maybe not every single second of the day, but enough that my husband has noticed it, and honestly that makes it feel more real because I don’t always catch it myself in the moment.

He’s said a few times that I move around the house like I’m underwater, which sounds dramatic, but I know what he means. Like I’ll be walking from the kitchen to the living room or trying to put something away, and it’s like everything takes more effort than it should. I’m not necessarily trying to move slowly, but my body just doesn’t seem to respond at a normal pace.

And when he asks me something simple — like, “Did you call so-and-so?” or “Where did you put that thing?” — it can take me a while to answer. I hear him, I understand the question, but the words just don’t come out right away. It’s almost like there’s a delay between thinking and speaking. And then when I do answer, it comes out slowly, like I’m dragging the words out.

It’s embarrassing, actually, because it makes me feel kind of dull or checked out, even if I’m trying to be present. So, yes, I have felt stuck in that slowed-down kind of state lately.

Some nights I know I’m tired, and I’ll tell myself I should go to bed, but I don’t always have a good sense of whether I’m actually sleeping badly or just feeling worn out in general.\n\nI haven’t noticed some big, obvious pattern that I could point to, like, “Oh, every night I’m awake until this exact time,” or anything like that. It’s more that my days feel kind of blurred lately, so when you ask about sleep I have to stop and think, “Wait, am I sleeping okay?” And I honestly don’t know. I might be sleeping less or not sleeping deeply, but I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed anything really clear with my appetite. I haven’t been tracking meals or weight or anything like that, so I’d be guessing a little.\n\nSo… maybe it’s a bit off, but I can’t point to a definite change like “I’m eating a lot more” or “I’m barely eating.” It’s more that I haven’t really been noticing food much either way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about not wanting to be alive or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Looking back, it was a little much. I wasn’t calmly working through a list. It feels like everything takes extra effort, even just walking from one room to another or getting something from the kitchen. I’ll realize I’ve been standing there for a second before actually doing the next thing, like my body is delayed.\n\nAnd when he asks me something simple, like what I want for dinner or whether I saw where something is, sometimes it takes me a while to answer. Over the past two months I haven’t noticed my weight changing in any major way.\n\nI’m not someone who checks the scale consistently, so I can’t give you an exact number or say, “Oh, I lost five pounds,” or anything like that. I mostly notice through my clothes or if someone comments, and nothing has really stood out. My clothes fit about the same, as far as I can tell.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not completely sure, but I haven’t noticed any significant weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been fidgeting or having trouble staying still when sitting down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I know I kind of touched on this already, but it really was like I could not stay in the chair for more than a few minutes at a time.\n\nI’d sit down thinking I was going to do one thing, and then suddenly I’d be up again because I had this idea that I needed to sketch out a different office layout, like where desks or shelves should go, even though nobody had asked me to do that and there wasn’t any real reason it had to be handled right then. Then I’d sit down again, and almost immediately I’d think, no, I should reorganize these files first, or I should move this stack over there, or start some other little task.\n\nIt wasn’t just normal fidgeting, like tapping my foot. And at the time it felt like I was being productive, but looking back, I was just bouncing from one thing to another and couldn’t really stay still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're resting or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. He’ll ask me something very basic, like what I want for dinner or whether I saw some email, and I know I heard him, but it takes me a while to answer. It’s like the words are there somewhere, but they come out late and slow, and then I feel bad because he’s waiting and I can tell he’s trying not to get impatient.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have noticed it more because he’s noticed it. It’s not all the time every second, but lately it’s been enough that it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about not enjoying the things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel guilty, though I don’t know if it’s only about not enjoying things. It sort of turns into this bigger guilt spiral.\n\nLike, if I’m not enjoying something we used to do, or I’m just not able to be present for it, my mind goes straight back to the mortgage. I keep replaying that decision over and over, like I’m reviewing evidence against myself. I tell myself I was selfish, that I pushed us into something reckless, that I put our whole household at risk because I wanted things to move forward or because I thought I knew what I was doing.\n\nAnd my husband keeps reminding me, “We made that decision together,” which is objectively true. I know that. I can hear him say it, and part of me understands it. But emotionally it doesn’t land. I still feel like somehow it’s my fault, like I should have been the one to stop it or question it more or protect us from the pressure of it.\n\nSo when I’m not enjoying normal things, it just adds another layer. I think, great, not only did I put us in this position, now I can’t even be a decent partner and enjoy our life. It makes me feel ashamed, honestly. Like I’ve failed at something I’m supposed to be able to handle.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, so I could be off by a little. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, and nobody has said anything like, “Oh, you’ve lost weight,” so I don’t think there’s been some major drop.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure what counts as significant, but no, I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly lost a lot of weight recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… actually, yes. There was this burst where I really could not sit still. It wasn’t just normal “I should get things done” energy, it felt like I kept popping up out of my chair before I’d even finished whatever I was doing.\n\nI remember I kept jumping up to sketch out different office layouts, like I suddenly needed to figure out where everything should go, even though there was no real deadline or reason it had to be solved right then. Then I’d start reorganizing files, and before that was even finished I’d think of one more thing I should do, and then one more thing after that.\n\nIt was kind of scattered, but at the time it felt productive, like I was finally going to get everything in order. Looking back, it was a little much. I wasn’t calmly working through a list. I was just… up and down, up and down, starting tasks because I felt like I had to, not because they were actually urgent.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. My husband has actually pointed it out more than I have noticed it myself. He’ll say I’m moving around the house like I’m underwater, which is… embarrassing, but also kind of accurate.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing to move slowly. It feels like everything takes extra effort, even just walking from one room to another or getting something from the kitchen. I’ll realize I’ve been standing there for a second before actually doing the next thing, like my body is delayed.\n\nAnd when he asks me something simple, like what I want for dinner or whether I saw where something is, sometimes it takes me a while to answer. I know I should be able to just respond, but the words come out slowly, or I have to kind of gather them first. He’ll be waiting, and I can see him trying to be patient, but I know it’s noticeable.\n\nSo yes, most of the time lately I do seem slower — moving slower, talking slower — and it’s frustrating because inside I’m aware of it, but I can’t always make myself speed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly point to, no. I mostly notice through my clothes or if someone comments, and nothing has really stood out. I used to love taking bird photos, and now I’ll see it there and think I should take it out, but it feels like too much effort for something that probably won’t feel worth it anyway.\n\nSo… I guess there are things I used to enjoy, but lately I’m not really finding pleasure in them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that clearly. I don’t think I’ve been tracking it very well. Some nights I know I’m tired, and I’ll tell myself I should go to bed, but I don’t always have a good sense of whether I’m actually sleeping badly or just feeling worn out in general.\n\nI haven’t noticed some big, obvious pattern that I could point to, like, “Oh, every night I’m awake until this exact time,” or anything like that. It’s more that my days feel kind of blurred lately, so when you ask about sleep I have to stop and think, “Wait, am I sleeping okay?” And I honestly don’t know. I might be sleeping less or not sleeping deeply, but I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed anything really clear with my appetite. It’s not something I’ve been paying close attention to, honestly.\n\nI mean, I eat, but some days it’s more just because it’s time to eat or because I realize I haven’t had anything in a while, not necessarily because I’m hungry in a normal way. But then other times I’ll snack on whatever’s easy, so it’s hard for me to say there’s a real pattern. I haven’t been tracking meals or weight or anything like that, so I’d be guessing a little.\n\nSo… maybe it’s a bit off, but I can’t point to a definite change like “I’m eating a lot more” or “I’m barely eating.” It’s more that I haven’t really been noticing food much either way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about not wanting to be alive or wishing you could just disappear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve sat down and made some kind of plan or anything like that. I haven’t. But late at night, especially when everything is quiet and I’m just lying there with my thoughts, I’ll catch myself thinking that my husband might be better off without me. And then I sort of scare myself because it’s not just a random one-time thought. It comes back.\n\nSometimes it’s more like wishing I could just disappear or not have to be here dealing with myself, if that makes sense. And then I feel guilty for even thinking it, because I know how awful that would be for him, but in the moment my brain keeps going there anyway. So… yes, I’ve been having those thoughts, and the fact that they keep returning is what worries me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I think it really started around when we took on the mortgage… so, about two months ago now. That’s the point I keep coming back to when I try to make sense of it.\n\nBefore that, I was kind of in this very overcharged mode about work and business stuff, like pushing hard and feeling like I had to keep everything moving. And then after the mortgage happened, it’s like I swung the other way. I don’t know if it was the pressure of it or just the reality setting in, but since then my mood has been in this low stretch.\n\nSo… not forever, but not just a couple bad days either. About two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I didn’t really notice it at first, or maybe I didn’t want to notice it, but my husband has actually pointed it out a few times.\n\nHe’ll say I’m moving around the house like I’m underwater, which sounds dramatic, but I know what he means. Like I’ll get up to do something simple, even just walking from one room to another or putting something away, and I feel like everything takes this ridiculous amount of effort. I’m not rushing anywhere. I’m just sort of… slow. Heavy, almost.\n\nAnd with talking, that’s been happening too. He’ll ask me something very basic, like what I want for dinner or whether I saw some email, and I know I heard him, but it takes me a while to answer. It’s like the words are there somewhere, but they come out late and slow, and then I feel bad because he’s waiting and I can tell he’s trying not to get impatient.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have noticed it more because he’s noticed it. It’s not all the time every second, but lately it’s been enough that it feels real.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about not enjoying the things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel guilty, though I don’t know if it’s only about not enjoying things. It sort of turns into this bigger guilt spiral.\n\nLike, if I’m not enjoying something we used to do, or I’m just not able to be present for it, my mind goes straight back to the mortgage. I keep replaying that decision over and over, like I’m reviewing evidence against myself. I tell myself I was selfish, that I pushed us into something reckless, that I put our whole household at risk because I wanted things to move forward or because I thought I knew what I was doing.\n\nAnd my husband keeps reminding me, “We made that decision together,” which is objectively true. I know that. I can hear him say it, and part of me understands it. But emotionally it doesn’t land. I still feel like somehow it’s my fault, like I should have been the one to stop it or question it more or protect us from the pressure of it.\n\nSo when I’m not enjoying normal things, it just adds another layer. I think, great, not only did I put us in this position, now I can’t even be a decent partner and enjoy our life. It makes me feel ashamed, honestly. Like I’ve failed at something I’m supposed to be able to handle.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a significant amount, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, so I could be off by a little. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, and nobody has said anything like, “Oh, you’ve lost weight,” so I don’t think there’s been some major drop.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure what counts as significant, but no, I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly lost a lot of weight recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… actually, yes. There was this burst where I really could not sit still. It wasn’t just normal “I should get things done” energy, it felt like I kept popping up out of my chair before I’d even finished whatever I was doing.\n\nI remember I kept jumping up to sketch out different office layouts, like I suddenly needed to figure out where everything should go, even though there was no real deadline or reason it had to be solved right then. Then I’d start reorganizing files, and before that was even finished I’d think of one more thing I should do, and then one more thing after that.\n\nIt was kind of scattered, but at the time it felt productive, like I was finally going to get everything in order. Looking back, it was a little much. I wasn’t calmly working through a list. I was just… up and down, up and down, starting tasks because I felt like I had to, not because they were actually urgent.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. My husband has actually pointed it out more than I have noticed it myself. He’ll say I’m moving around the house like I’m underwater, which is… embarrassing, but also kind of accurate.\n\nIt’s not like I’m choosing to move slowly. It feels like everything takes extra effort, even just walking from one room to another or getting something from the kitchen. I’ll realize I’ve been standing there for a second before actually doing the next thing, like my body is delayed.\n\nAnd when he asks me something simple, like what I want for dinner or whether I saw where something is, sometimes it takes me a while to answer. I know I should be able to just respond, but the words come out slowly, or I have to kind of gather them first. Yes, I think so… though for me it didn’t feel like just fidgeting or pacing for no reason. It was more like I couldn’t leave things alone, like I had to be doing something to move this business idea forward right then.

I know I should be able to just respond, but the words come out slowly, or I have to kind of gather them first. He’ll be waiting, and I can see him trying to be patient, but I know it’s noticeable.\n\nSo yes, most of the time lately I do seem slower — moving slower, talking slower — and it’s frustrating because inside I’m aware of it, but I can’t always make myself speed up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Not that I can clearly point to, no. Over the past two months I haven’t noticed my weight changing in any major way.\n\nI’m not someone who checks the scale consistently, so I can’t give you an exact number or say, “Oh, I lost five pounds,” or anything like that. My clothes fit about the same, as far as I can tell.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not completely sure, but I haven’t noticed any significant weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been fidgeting or having trouble staying still when sitting down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I know I kind of touched on this already, but it really was like I could not stay in the chair for more than a few minutes at a time.\n\nI’d sit down thinking I was going to do one thing, and then suddenly I’d be up again because I had this idea that I needed to sketch out a different office layout, like where desks or shelves should go, even though nobody had asked me to do that and there wasn’t any real reason it had to be handled right then. Then I’d sit down again, and almost immediately I’d think, no, I should reorganize these files first, or I should move this stack over there, or start some other little task.\n\nIt wasn’t just normal fidgeting, like tapping my foot. And then when I do answer, it comes out slowly, like I’m dragging the words out.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, actually, because it makes me feel kind of dull or checked out, even if I’m trying to be present. So, yes, I have felt stuck in that slowed-down kind of state lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like your thoughts are racing or your mind is overly active lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed my thoughts racing in the sense of my mind going really fast or jumping ahead of me.\n\nSometimes I do get caught up thinking about things, but I don’t know if that’s “overly active” or just normal worrying or trying to figure things out. It hasn’t stood out to me lately as this clear feeling of my brain speeding up. If anything, I’d have to think about what you mean by racing thoughts, because I’m not sure I’m recognizing that in myself right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any restlessness or agitation that makes it hard for you to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though for me it didn’t feel like just fidgeting or pacing for no reason.
